Their determinant lines use the determinant-of-cohomology formalism of Knudsen and Mumford <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[KnudsenMumford76](#ref-KnudsenMumford76)]; the functorial construction and its compatibility with pullback are recalled in <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Tags 0FJI and 0FJW]. Each factor in $\mathcal D$ is the inverse Deligne pairing of $A_m,V_m$ tensored with the fixed line $\det R\Gamma_C(\mathcal O_C)$ <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Deligne87](#ref-Deligne87), Construction 7.2, Equation (7.2.1)]. We keep the full determinant expression when defining transport.

<a id="stmt-lem-determinant-descent"></a>
<div id="lem:determinant-descent" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *The sheaf $\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal D$ on $Z\times X\times R$ has a genuine $H$-equivariant structure commuting with $\Delta_0$. Its derived restriction to $R=0$ defines an equivariant coherent class $\beta$ on $Z\times X$. Nonequivariantly this class is the tensor product of $\mathcal D$ with a class pulled back from $B\times X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-determinant-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-determinant-descent</summary>

*Proof.* Write $g_C$ for the genus of $C$. For one pair $A,V$ of degrees zero and one, $$\chi(A)=1-g_C,\qquad
\chi(A\otimes V)=\chi(V)=2-g_C.$$ A scalar acting on a line inside a determinant of cohomology has exponent equal to its Euler characteristic. Thus the corresponding factor of $\mathcal D$ has scalar weights $$\bigl(-\chi(A\otimes V)+\chi(A),
      -\chi(A\otimes V)+\chi(V)\bigr)=(-1,0)$$ in $A,V$. The first weight cancels the weight one of $\mathcal F$, and the second leaves no dependence on identifications of $V$. Local choices of line identifications therefore give the same transport on $\mathcal F\otimes\mathcal D$. To check the group law, compare the two successive transports with the composed identifications of the universal lines. Composition in $J_g\otimes g_*J_h\longrightarrow J_{gh}$ is the associative composition from $g_*h_*A$ to $g_*A$ to $A$. Functoriality of determinants and the genuine transport of the original module identify the two maps for these composed choices. Any scalar change of the $A$-identification contributes opposite weights on $\mathcal F$ and $\mathcal D$, and a change of the $V$-identification has weight zero. Thus no scalar cocycle remains, and the maps glue to genuine equivariance.

The origin of the representation $R$ is $H$-fixed. Its immersion in the product has the finite equivariant Koszul resolution on the linear coordinates of $R$, even when $X$ is singular. The alternating coherent Tor class of restriction therefore defines $\beta$, preserving both actions. Since $\mathcal F$ does not depend on $P$, the last assertion follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Extracting the reduced rank

<a id="eq:beta-fiber-factorization"></a>
Fix a complex embedding. All varieties in this paragraph are base changed along it. The action of $H$ is now $\mathbb C$-linear and fixes the constant algebra $\Delta_0\otimes_k\mathbb C$ pointwise. Consequently taking the image under right multiplication by $\epsilon$ is exact and preserves equivariance. It divides the dimension of every finite-dimensional matrix module by $e_0$. The product $B\times X_{\mathrm{sm}}\times R$ is smooth and connected. On it, $\mathcal F$ is perfect, and the alternating rank of a locally free resolution is constantly $e_0r$. Derived restriction to $R=0$ preserves this rank. Thus, for each $x\in X_{\mathrm{sm}}$, Lemma <a href="#lem:determinant-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:determinant-descent">21</a> gives $$\label{eq:beta-fiber-factorization}
\beta'|_{Z\times x}=\operatorname{pr}_B^*\gamma_x\otimes\mathcal D,
\qquad \gamma_x\in K^0(B),\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\gamma_x=r.$$ This argument uses derived restriction; no flatness of $\mathcal F$ over $R$ is required.

<a id="stmt-lem-mixed-picard-pairing"></a>
<div id="lem:mixed-picard-pairing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22**. *For any class $\gamma\in K^0(B)$, $$\int_Z\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\operatorname{pr}_B^*\gamma\otimes\mathcal D)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(Z)
   =\pm\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\gamma,$$ where the sign is independent of $\gamma$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mixed-picard-pairing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mixed-picard-pairing</summary>

*Proof.* It suffices to calculate the determinant correction for one curve. Write $a=c_1(A)$, $v=c_1(V)$, and let $q$ project off $C$. Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch for this smooth proper projection gives $$c_1\!\left(\bigl(\det Rq_*(A\otimes V)\bigr)^{-1}
       \otimes\det Rq_*A\otimes\det Rq_*V\right)
       =-q_*(av).$$ Indeed the signed exponential sum $-e^{a+v}+e^a+e^v$ has degree-two part zero and degree-four part $-av$; a curve has no degree-four Todd term.

Normalization at the chosen curve point removes pure parameter-space degree-two components from both universal classes. The class $a$ has no pure curve component because $\deg A=0$. The pure curve component of $v$ cannot contribute to $q_*(av)$. The resulting class is therefore purely mixed between $H^1(\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(C),\mathbb Z)$ and $H^1(\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^1(C),\mathbb Z)$. Tensoring by $\mathcal O_C(-c_0)$, for the chosen curve point $c_0$, identifies the torsor $\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^1(C)$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(C)$; different choices change this identification by translation, which acts trivially on integral first cohomology. The universal classes identify these integral lattices with the curve’s first-cohomology lattice: pullback by an Abel map gives the mixed component of the diagonal class on $C\times C$, and the Abel map induces an integral $H^1$-isomorphism. The intersection form on $H^1(C,\mathbb Z)$ is unimodular. Hence the mixed class has a unimodular matrix $\Lambda$. This is the cohomological Poincaré pairing; the rank extraction below is the numerical counterpart of the Fourier–Mukai calculation in <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Mukai81](#ref-Mukai81), Theorem 2.2 and Example 2.6].

More explicitly, if $d=2g_C$ is the real dimension of either Picard factor and $b_i,p_j$ are integral bases, a mixed class $\theta=\sum_{i,j}\Lambda_{ij}b_i\wedge p_j$ satisfies $$\int_{\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(C)\times\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^1(C)}\frac{\theta^d}{d!}
       =\pm\det\Lambda=\pm1.$$ The factors for distinct curves form separate blocks, so the same unit calculation holds on $Z$. Each Picard tangent bundle is trivial, giving $\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(Z)=1$. To obtain top degree on $P$ from $\exp(c_1(\mathcal D))$, one must already use top degree on $B$, because every mixed term has degree one on each side. Thus every positive-degree term of $\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\gamma)$ contributes zero. Only its rank remains. ◻

</details>

Combining <a href="#eq:beta-fiber-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:beta-fiber-factorization">[eq:beta-fiber-factorization]</a> with Lemma <a href="#lem:mixed-picard-pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixed-picard-pairing">22</a> proves <a href="#eq:descent-fiber-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-fiber-index">[eq:descent-fiber-index]</a>. It remains to obtain the arithmetic divisibility of the twists over $k$, where $\Delta_0$ is still division. We need an Euler-characteristic fact that also applies to the singular variety $X$.

<a id="stmt-lem-torsor-euler"></a>
<div id="lem:torsor-euler" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23**. *Let $Q$ be a projective scheme over a field, and let $\delta:T'\to Q$ be a torsor under a constant finite group of order $a$. For every coherent-sheaf class $\alpha$ on $Q$, $$\chi(T',\delta^*\alpha)=a\,\chi(Q,\alpha).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-torsor-euler"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-torsor-euler</summary>

*Proof.* The vector bundle $\delta_*\mathcal O_{T'}$ has rank $a$. Put $d=[\delta_*\mathcal O_{T'}]-a\in K^0(Q)$. Multiplication by $d$ lowers the dimension-of-support filtration on the coherent-sheaf group $G_0(Q)$. To see this, the successive quotients of that filtration are generated by integral closed supports <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Lemma 42.23.2, Tag 02S9]; on an integral support the two bundles in $d$ have the same rank and are isomorphic on a dense open set. Localization therefore puts their difference in smaller support dimension. In particular this operator is nilpotent.

The torsor becomes the disjoint union of $a$ copies of $T'$ after pullback to $T'$, so $\delta^*d=0$. The projection formula gives, as operators on $G_0(Q)$, $$(a+d)d=0.$$ Over $\mathbb Q$, the operator $a+d$ is invertible by a finite nilpotent expansion, hence $d$ acts by zero. Euler characteristic takes values in $\mathbb Z$, so it kills torsion and $\chi(Q,d\alpha)=0$. Finite pushforward and the projection formula now give the claimed equality. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-torsor-euler-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-torsor-euler</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:rank-to-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rank-to-euler">19</a>.* The construction and the first identity have been established above. By Proposition <a href="#prop:binary-curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:binary-curves">13</a>, $H$ acts freely on $P$, hence on $Z\times X$. Its quotient is a projective scheme, and the quotient morphism is a finite étale $H$-torsor; existence of the scheme quotient and torsor follows from <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Tag 07S7], and a product of the translates of an ample line bundle gives projectivity of the quotient. Equivariant coherent sheaves descend along this torsor, together with their commuting $\Delta_0$-actions <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Proposition 35.5.2, Tag 023R]. The same holds for $\beta\otimes\mathcal O_X(l)$, since $\mathcal O_X(1)$ is linearized.

Each cohomology group of a descended sheaf is a finite right module over the division algebra $\Delta_0/k$, so its $k$-dimension is divisible by $e_0^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:torsor-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:torsor-euler">23</a> multiplies its Euler characteristic by $|H|=e_1$ on passing upstairs. Applying this term by term to the coherent class gives $$\chi(Z\times X,\beta\otimes\mathcal O_X(l))\in e_0^2e_1\mathbb Z,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:descent-twist-divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-twist-divisibility">[eq:descent-twist-divisibility]</a>. Finally, field extension preserves coherent cohomology dimensions by flat base change <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks), Tag 02KH], even for a complex embedding that does not fix the original constants. Over $\mathbb C$, idempotent reduction divides these dimensions by $e_0$, proving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

We have obtained an Euler characteristic equal to the reduced rank up to sign, together with divisibility for every algebraic twist. The geometry of $X$ prevents an immediate comparison of these two statements. The next section makes that comparison using the connectivity of $Y^o$, and completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a>.

<a id="sec:index"></a>
### The equivariant index calculation

We complete the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a>. Retain its notation, and let $r$ be the reduced rank of a finite right module over $$\Delta=(\Delta _0\otimes_k F)\otimes_F\Delta _1.$$ Our objective is to prove $e_0e_1\mid r$. Proposition <a href="#prop:rank-to-euler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rank-to-euler">19</a> supplies an $H$-equivariant coherent class $\beta$ on $Z\times X$, and its reduction $\beta'$ after a complex embedding of $k$. It also supplies the two numerical identities <a href="#eq:descent-fiber-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-fiber-index">[eq:descent-fiber-index]</a> and <a href="#eq:descent-twist-divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-twist-divisibility">[eq:descent-twist-divisibility]</a>. The first recovers $r$ from an Euler characteristic on $Z$; the second gives divisibility of Euler characteristics on $Z\times X$. We connect them by replacing low dimensional tests in $X$ with projective space.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Equivariant maps into the smooth parameter space

Put $\mathbb T=(S^1)^M$. The point characters defining the action on the $M$ blocks of the cone give a homomorphism $\rho:H\longrightarrow\mathbb T$; write $H'=\rho(H)$. The group $\mathbb T$ need not preserve the cone, but its finite subgroup $H'$ does. The $H'$-action and scalar multiplication by $S^1$ commute on the unit cone $$Y_1=\{y\in Y^o:\|y\|=1\}.$$ Here the norm is the standard Hermitian norm after the chosen complex embedding. Radial retraction from $Y^o$ onto $Y_1$ is equivariant for both actions. By Theorem <a href="#thm:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:parameters">12</a>, $Y_1$ is $(M+2h)$-connected.

<a id="stmt-lem-index-equivariant-map"></a>
<div id="lem:index-equivariant-map" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24**. *There is an $H'$-equivariant map $$\Phi:\mathbb T\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)\longrightarrow X_{\mathrm{sm}},$$ where $H'$ acts by translation on $\mathbb T$ and trivially on $\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$. It has an $H'\times S^1$-equivariant lift $$\widetilde\Phi:\mathbb T\times S^{2h+1}\longrightarrow Y_1.$$ If $t_0\in\mathbb T$ is fixed and $\Phi_0(z)=\Phi(t_0,z)$, then $\Phi$ is homotopic, as an ordinary map, to $\Phi_0$ composed with projection onto $\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$, and $$\Phi_0^*\mathcal O_X(-1)\simeq\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^h}(-1).$$ There is also an $H$-equivariant map $\vartheta:P\longrightarrow\mathbb T$, for the action through $\rho$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-index-equivariant-map"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-index-equivariant-map</summary>

*Proof.* The product of the principal bundles $\mathbb T\to\mathbb T/H'$ and $S^{2h+1}\to\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$ is a principal $H'\times S^1$-bundle. An equivariant lift $\widetilde\Phi$ is a section of its associated bundle with fiber $Y_1$. The base $(\mathbb T/H')\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$ has a finite CW structure of dimension $M+2h$. Extend a section cell by cell: over an $a$-cell the extension obstruction is a map $S^{a-1}\to Y_1$, which is null-homotopic by the connectivity just established. The initial choice over zero-cells and extension over one-cells are allowed because $Y_1$ is nonempty and path connected. This constructs $\widetilde\Phi$, and passage to the scalar-circle quotient gives $\Phi$.

To compare $\Phi$ with $\Phi_0$, forget $H'$-equivariance. The lifts $\widetilde\Phi(t,z)$ and $\widetilde\Phi(t_0,z)$ are sections of the same circle-associated bundle over $\mathbb T\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$. Construct a homotopy relative to its two endpoints by extending over cells of the base times $[0,1]$. This relative CW pair has dimension $M+2h+1$, so the largest obstruction belongs to $\pi_{M+2h}(Y_1)$, which also vanishes. Projectivizing this homotopy proves the assertion about $\Phi$. The circle-equivariant map $S^{2h+1}\to Y_1$ obtained at $t_0$ identifies the associated tautological line bundles, giving the stated isomorphism for $\Phi_0$.

Finally, any character of $H=G^{M-1}$ is a product of characters of its factors. Lemma <a href="#lem:picard-character-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:picard-character-maps">17</a> realizes each factor character by an equivariant circle-valued map on the corresponding factor of $P$. Multiplying these maps realizes each coordinate of $\rho$. Their product is $\vartheta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### An integral family of indices

To bring $\beta'$ to projective space, use the maps just constructed. Writing $z=(a,p)\in B\times P=Z$, define $$\gamma:Z\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)\longrightarrow Z\times X_{\mathrm{sm}},
 \qquad
 \gamma(z,t)=(z,\Phi(\vartheta(p),t)).$$ This map is $H$-equivariant, with trivial action on $\mathbb P^h$. Since $H$ acts freely on $Z$, the quotient $V=Z/H$ is smooth projective, and $\gamma$ induces a map $$V\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)\longrightarrow (Z\times X_{\mathrm{sm}})/H.$$ The ordinary homotopy from $\Phi$ to $\Phi_0$ will later compare this pullback with an algebraic linear section. For descent we use $\gamma$ itself, because $\Phi_0$ need not be equivariant. These maps are continuous, so the pullback class need not be algebraic. We therefore need integration in topological $K$-theory that retains integer coefficients, beyond the rational Chern-character formula.

<a id="eq:index-projective-k"></a>
We use $K^0(T)$ for the Grothendieck group of complex topological vector bundles on a compact space $T$. The group extends to the noncompact smooth varieties below by the same vector-bundle definition; a coherent class on such a variety defines a class in this group by a finite locally free resolution. On $\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$, put $$u=1-[\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^h}(-1)]\in K^0(\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)).$$ The projective bundle formula gives $$\label{eq:index-projective-k}
 K^0(\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C))=\mathbb Z[u]/(u^{h+1}).$$ In particular, the coefficients in this basis are integers, a fact we need in addition to the rational Chern character.

The integrality statement below is a special case of the differentiable Riemann–Roch theorem of Atiyah and Hirzebruch <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[AtiyahHirzebruch59](#ref-AtiyahHirzebruch59), Theorem 1 and Remark (2)]. We give the projective-space argument with an explicit choice of Thom class.

<a id="stmt-lem-index-integral-pushforward"></a>
<div id="lem:index-integral-pushforward" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *Let $V$ be a smooth complex projective variety and let $\xi\in K^0(V\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C))$. There is a unique $I_V(\xi)\in K^0(\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C))$ such that <a id="eq:index-integral-pushforward"></a>$$\label{eq:index-integral-pushforward}
 \mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(I_V(\xi))
   =\int_V\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\xi)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(T_V).$$ The integral on the right is integration over the $V$-factor.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-index-integral-pushforward"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-index-integral-pushforward</summary>

<a id="eq:index-ambient-integration"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a projective embedding $i:V\hookrightarrow\mathbb P^a(\mathbb C)$, with complex normal bundle $\nu$. The complex $K$-theory Thom class, followed by extension from a tubular neighborhood, defines $$i_!:K^0(V\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C))
        \longrightarrow K^0(\mathbb P^a(\mathbb C)\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)).$$ We use the Koszul convention for this Thom class: its restriction to the zero section of $\nu$ is $\lambda_{-1}(\nu^\vee)$. If $x_1,\ldots,x_c$ are the Chern roots of $\nu$, its Chern character, divided by the ordinary cohomological Thom class, is $$\prod_{\alpha=1}^c\frac{1-e^{-x_\alpha}}{x_\alpha}
       =\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(\nu)^{-1}.$$ For completeness, this identity follows on the zero section from $\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\lambda_{-1}(\nu^\vee))=\prod_\alpha(1-e^{-x_\alpha})$ and from the Euler class $\prod_\alpha x_\alpha$. The splitting principle verifies the identity universally for sums of line bundles, where the product of the universal Chern classes is a non-zero-divisor; the Thom isomorphism then gives the asserted identity before restriction as well. Consequently $$\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(i_!\xi)=i_*\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\xi)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(\nu)^{-1}\bigr).$$ The exact tangent-normal sequence now implies $$\label{eq:index-ambient-integration}
 \int_{\mathbb P^a}\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(i_!\xi)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(T_{\mathbb P^a})
    =\int_V\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\xi)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(T_V).$$

The integral projective bundle formula identifies $$K^0(\mathbb P^a(\mathbb C)\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C))
    =\mathbb Z[v,u]/(v^{a+1},u^{h+1}),\qquad
 v=1-[\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^a}(-1)].$$ Write $i_!\xi=\sum_{i=0}^a\sum_{j=0}^h c_{ij}v^iu^j$, with $c_{ij}\in\mathbb Z$. The number $$\int_{\mathbb P^a}\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(v^i)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(T_{\mathbb P^a})
    =\chi\bigl(\mathbb P^a,(1-[\mathcal O(-1)])^i\bigr)$$ is an integer by Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch, applied additively to the finite sum of line-bundle classes $v^i$. Thus the left side of <a href="#eq:index-ambient-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-ambient-integration">[eq:index-ambient-integration]</a> is the Chern character of an integral combination of $1,u,\ldots,u^h$, which gives $I_V(\xi)$. Uniqueness follows because the Chern character is injective on the torsion-free group in <a href="#eq:index-projective-k" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-projective-k">[eq:index-projective-k]</a>: if $H=c_1(\mathcal O(1))$, then $\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(u)=1-e^{-H}$ has leading term $H$, so these $h+1$ Chern characters are linearly independent over $\mathbb Q$. The projective bundle and Thom constructions used here are standard complex $K$-theory, with the Koszul convention for the Thom class specified above <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[Hatcher](#ref-Hatcher), Proposition 2.24, Theorem 2.25, and pp. 113–114]; for the Riemann–Roch formula see <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[BorelSerre58](#ref-BorelSerre58), 7]. ◻

</details>

For a projective variety and a virtual coherent or algebraic vector-bundle class, $\chi$ will always mean the additive Euler characteristic of that class. We now apply the preceding lemma to the equivariant class from Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-index-test"></a>
<div id="prop:index-test" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:index-section-divisibility"></a>
**Proposition 26**. *In the setting of Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a>, let $r$ be the reduced rank of any finite right $\Delta$-module. There is a smooth $h$-dimensional linear section $D_0\subset X_{\mathrm{sm}}$, a complete intersection of $s$ hypersurfaces of degree $b$ in $\mathbb P^{h+s}(\mathbb C)$, and integers $q_0,\ldots,q_h\in e_1\mathbb Z$ such that $q_0=\pm r$ and $$\label{eq:index-section-divisibility}
 \sum_{j=0}^h q_j\,
 \chi\!\left(D_0,
       (1-[\mathcal O_{D_0}(-1)])^j\otimes\mathcal O_{D_0}(l)\right)
       \in e_0e_1\mathbb Z
       \qquad(l\in\mathbb Z).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-index-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-index-test</summary>

*Proof.* Pull back the topological class of $\beta'|_{Z\times X_{\mathrm{sm}}}$ along $\gamma$ and call the resulting class $\sigma$. This pullback is permitted because the class is perfect on the smooth open product.

<a id="eq:index-family"></a>
The equivariant coherent class $\beta'$ descends on the free smooth quotient $(Z\times X_{\mathrm{sm}})/H$. That quotient is smooth quasi-projective, so the descended coherent class has a topological vector-bundle class. Pulling it back along the quotient of $\gamma$ gives $\tau\in K^0(V\times\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C))$ whose pullback is $\sigma$. Since $Z\to V$ is an étale covering of degree $e_1$ and $T_Z$ is the pullback of $T_V$, Lemma <a href="#lem:index-integral-pushforward" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:index-integral-pushforward">25</a> gives $$\label{eq:index-family}
 \int_Z\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(\sigma)\mathop{\mathrm{td}}(T_Z)
   =e_1\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}(I_V(\tau))
   =\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}\!\left(\sum_{j=0}^h q_j u^j\right),
 \qquad q_j\in e_1\mathbb Z.$$

Nonequivariantly, Lemma <a href="#lem:index-equivariant-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:index-equivariant-map">24</a> makes $\gamma$ homotopic to $(z,t)\mapsto(z,\Phi_0(t))$. The equality <a href="#eq:index-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-family">[eq:index-family]</a> therefore computes the same fiber integral for this latter pullback of $\beta'$. Evaluate it at any point of $\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C)$. Only $q_0$ remains on the right, whereas <a href="#eq:descent-fiber-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-fiber-index">[eq:descent-fiber-index]</a> gives $\pm r$ on the left. Thus $q_0=\pm r$.

This already gives $e_1\mid r$. The stronger divisibility by $e_0e_1$ is encoded in the algebraic twist indices $p(l)$ of <a href="#eq:descent-twist-divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-twist-divisibility">[eq:descent-twist-divisibility]</a>. A smooth linear section of $X$ retains that divisibility and can also be compared with the projective-space test. Put $d=\dim X$. Theorem <a href="#thm:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:parameters">12</a> gives $\operatorname{codim}_X(X\setminus X_{\mathrm{sm}})>h$. A general linear section of codimension $d-h$ therefore misses the singular locus; Bertini gives a smooth section $D_0$. As $X$ is a complete intersection of $s$ degree-$b$ equations, $D_0$ has the description in the statement.

Choose $h+1$ linear forms with no common zero on $D_0$; these exist by general position since $\dim D_0=h$. They define a morphism $$f:D_0\longrightarrow\mathbb P^h(\mathbb C),
 \qquad f^*\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^h}(-1)\simeq\mathcal O_{D_0}(-1).$$ The inclusion $j:D_0\hookrightarrow X_{\mathrm{sm}}$ and $\Phi_0\circ f$ have circle-equivariant lifts from the unit frame bundle of this same line bundle to $Y_1$. Indeed, choose the indicated line-bundle isomorphisms and normalize them by their positive fiber norms to identify the unit frame bundles. The relative section-extension argument in Lemma <a href="#lem:index-equivariant-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:index-equivariant-map">24</a>, now over $D_0\times[0,1]$, makes those lifts homotopic: the real dimension of this base is $2h+1$, below the available connectivity bound plus one. Thus $j$ and $\Phi_0\circ f$ are homotopic.

<a id="eq:index-section-rr"></a>
Pulling <a href="#eq:index-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-family">[eq:index-family]</a> back by $f$ now identifies the fiber integral on $Z\times D_0$ with $$\mathop{\mathrm{ch}}\!\left(
       \sum_{j=0}^h q_j(1-[\mathcal O_{D_0}(-1)])^j
     \right).
 \tag{\(\ast\)}$$ Define $$p_{D_0}(l)=
 \chi\!\left(Z\times D_0,
       \beta'|_{Z\times D_0}\otimes\mathcal O_{D_0}(l)\right),$$ where restriction of a coherent class is derived. Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch on the smooth projective product, followed by $(\ast)$, gives $$\label{eq:index-section-rr}
 p_{D_0}(l)=
 \sum_{j=0}^h q_j\,
 \chi\!\left(D_0,
       (1-[\mathcal O_{D_0}(-1)])^j\otimes\mathcal O_{D_0}(l)\right).$$

<a id="eq:index-koszul-difference"></a>
It remains to retain the original divisibility when passing to this section. Recall from <a href="#eq:descent-twist-divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-twist-divisibility">[eq:descent-twist-divisibility]</a> the integers $$p(l)=\chi(Z\times X,\beta\otimes\mathcal O_X(l))/e_0
       \in e_0e_1\mathbb Z.$$ The $d-h$ hyperplanes defining $D_0$ have a Koszul resolution on $X$: along $D_0$ they cut transversely in the smooth locus, and off their common zero locus the complex is exact because one of the equations is locally a unit. Tensoring this resolution derivedly with $\beta$, and then passing to the complex embedding and the matrix-idempotent reduction, yields $$\label{eq:index-koszul-difference}
 p_{D_0}(l)
   =\sum_{a=0}^{d-h}(-1)^a\binom{d-h}{a}p(l-a)
   \in e_0e_1\mathbb Z.$$ Here cohomology commutes with the field extension, and the matrix-idempotent reduction divides every cohomology dimension by $e_0$. The particular hyperplanes need only be defined after that extension: the terms on the right of <a href="#eq:index-koszul-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-koszul-difference">[eq:index-koszul-difference]</a> are the original integer Euler characteristics. Combining <a href="#eq:index-section-rr" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-section-rr">[eq:index-section-rr]</a> and <a href="#eq:index-koszul-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-koszul-difference">[eq:index-koszul-difference]</a> proves <a href="#eq:index-section-divisibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-section-divisibility">[eq:index-section-divisibility]</a>. ◻

</details>

For the integers in Proposition <a href="#prop:index-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:index-test">26</a>, put $$Q_*(u)=\sum_{j=0}^h(q_j/e_1)u^j\in\mathbb Z[u].$$ Its constant coefficient is $Q_*(0)=\pm r/e_1$, and all its twisted Euler characteristics on $D_0$ are divisible by $e_0$. The remaining step extracts the same divisibility for $Q_*(0)$. The dimension $h$ was chosen precisely to allow the following finite truncation argument.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Truncated polynomial congruences

<a id="stmt-lem-index-padic-truncation"></a>
<div id="lem:index-padic-truncation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *Let $p$ be a prime, let $v\ge1$, and let $A,Q\in\mathbb Z[u]$. Suppose the reduction of $A$ modulo $p$ has exact order $\nu\ge0$ at $u=0$. If $h\ge v\nu$ and $$A(u)Q(u)=0\pmod{(p^v,u^{h+1})},$$ then $p^v\mid Q(0)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-index-padic-truncation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-index-padic-truncation</summary>

*Proof.* We successively construct integer polynomials $Q_i$, for $0\le i\le v$, with $Q_0=Q$, such that $$Q_i(0)=Q(0)/p^i,\qquad
 A Q_i=0\pmod{(p^{v-i},u^{h-i\nu+1})}
       \quad(0\le i<v).$$ Suppose $i<v$ and $Q_i$ has been constructed. Modulo $p$, write $A=u^\nu B$, where $B(0)\ne0$. The power series $B$ is invertible over $\mathbb Z/p$, so the displayed congruence forces every coefficient of $Q_i$ through degree $$m_i=h-(i+1)\nu$$ to be divisible by $p$. This degree is nonnegative because $h\ge v\nu$. Let $Q_{i+1}$ be the truncation of $Q_i$ through degree $m_i$, divided by $p$. It is an integer polynomial and has the required constant coefficient. Since $$Q_i-pQ_{i+1}\in u^{m_i+1}\mathbb Z[u],$$ restricting the old congruence to degrees at most $m_i$ gives $$p A Q_{i+1}=0\pmod{(p^{v-i},u^{m_i+1})}.$$ Coefficientwise division by $p$ gives the next congruence modulo $p^{v-i-1}$. At $i=v-1$, the construction shows directly that $Q(0)/p^{v-1}$ is divisible by $p$. This proves the conclusion. The same induction includes $\nu=0$, when no degrees are lost. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-index-padic-truncation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-index-padic-truncation</summary>

<a id="eq:index-hilbert-series"></a>
*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a>.* Use $D_0$, $q_0,\ldots,q_h$, and $Q_*$ just constructed for the chosen module. If $e_0=1$, then $q_0=\pm r\in e_1\mathbb Z$ already gives the required divisibility, so assume $e_0>1$. The Hilbert series of the coordinate ring of the complete intersection $D_0$ is $$\frac{(1-t^b)^s}{(1-t)^{h+s+1}}.$$ Euler characteristics of sufficiently positive twists agree with Hilbert functions. Moreover, multiplication by $(1-[\mathcal O(-1)])^j$ gives the same finite difference as multiplication of generating functions by $(1-t)^j$, apart from finitely many initial coefficients. The coefficients in these differences are integers. It follows from <a href="#eq:index-section-rr" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-section-rr">[eq:index-section-rr]</a> that $$\label{eq:index-hilbert-series}
 \sum_{l\ge0}\frac{p_{D_0}(l)}{e_1}t^l
   =Q_*(1-t)\frac{(1-t^b)^s}{(1-t)^{h+s+1}}+R(t),
 \qquad R(t)\in\mathbb Z[t].$$ By <a href="#eq:index-koszul-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-koszul-difference">[eq:index-koszul-difference]</a>, every coefficient on the left is divisible by $e_0$.

<a id="eq:index-final-congruence"></a>
Multiply <a href="#eq:index-hilbert-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-hilbert-series">[eq:index-hilbert-series]</a> by $(1-t)^{h+s+1}$ and reduce coefficients modulo $e_0$. The resulting right side is a polynomial that vanishes coefficientwise. We may therefore substitute $t=1-u$, obtaining $$Q_*(u)\bigl(1-(1-u)^b\bigr)^s
     =0\pmod{(e_0,u^{h+s+1})}.$$ Define $$A_b(u)=\frac{1-(1-u)^b}{u}\in\mathbb Z[u].$$ Cancelling the common monomial $u^s$ yields $$\label{eq:index-final-congruence}
 A_b(u)^sQ_*(u)=0\pmod{(e_0,u^{h+1})}.$$ This is coefficientwise cancellation of a monomial, valid over $\mathbb Z/e_0$ even when $e_0$ is composite. When $s=0$, the factor is $1$, and the constant coefficient of <a href="#eq:index-final-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-final-congruence">[eq:index-final-congruence]</a> immediately gives $e_0\mid Q_*(0)$.

For the general case, fix a prime $p\mid e_0$ and write $v=v_p(e_0)$, $b=p^a c$, where $p\nmid c$. Over $\mathbb Z/p$, $$1-(1-u)^b
   =1-(1-u^{p^a})^c
   =c\,u^{p^a}+\text{terms of higher degree}.$$ Thus $A_b(u)^s$ has exact order $\nu=s(p^a-1)$ modulo $p$, with nonzero leading coefficient. The choice of $h$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a> gives $h\ge v\nu$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:index-padic-truncation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:index-padic-truncation">27</a> to <a href="#eq:index-final-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:index-final-congruence">[eq:index-final-congruence]</a>, reduced modulo $p^v$, to obtain $p^v\mid Q_*(0)$. This also covers primes not dividing $b$, for which $\nu=0$; no assumption that $b$ is a prime power has been made. Doing this for every prime divisor of $e_0$ gives $$e_0\mid Q_*(0),\qquad
 e_0e_1\mid q_0=\pm r.$$ The module was arbitrary. A nonzero proper right ideal of the central simple algebra $\Delta$, whose degree is $e_0e_1$, would have reduced rank strictly between $0$ and $e_0e_1$. The divisibility just proved excludes such an ideal, so $\Delta$ is division. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Construction of the triangular power identities

We now construct one new triangular identity while preserving the low-degree relations from Section <a href="#sec:low-relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:low-relations">3</a>. The division theorem allows all the binary addition matrices to be used in the same coefficient algebra. The remaining tasks are to make the final output invariant and to keep the formal relation algebra finite over the old one.

We first record a binary addition using only linear and quadratic relations. The resulting algebra is finite over the input algebra; Lemma <a href="#lem:sparsity-preserved" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sparsity-preserved">10</a> will then preserve the condition that the characteristic set contains no curve of small degree.

<a id="stmt-lem-veronese-extension"></a>
<div id="lem:veronese-extension" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:veronese-exchanges"></a>
<a id="eq:veronese-linear"></a>
<a id="eq:veronese-monic"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Finite extension from the Veronese relations). *Let $b\ge2$, let $k$ contain a primitive $b$-th root of unity $\zeta$, and let $A$ be a commutative $k$-algebra with elements $u,v$. Introduce symbols $T_\alpha$, indexed by $\alpha\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^{b}$ with $|\alpha|=b$, and put $$D_j=T_{b e_j}\quad(0\le j<b),\qquad
 z=\frac1b\sum_{j=0}^{b-1}D_j,\qquad
 u_T=-\frac1b\sum_{j=0}^{b-1}\zeta^{-j}D_j,\qquad
 v_T=T_{(1,\ldots,1)}.$$ Let $A'$ be the quotient of $A[T_\alpha]$ by the relations $$\begin{aligned}
 T_\alpha T_\beta&=T_\gamma T_\delta
       &&\text{whenever }\alpha+\beta=\gamma+\delta,
       \label{eq:veronese-exchanges}\\
 D_j&=z-\zeta^j u_T
       &&(0\le j<b),\qquad u_T=u,\quad v_T=v.
       \label{eq:veronese-linear}
\end{aligned}$$ Then $A'$ is finite over $A$, and in $A'$ $$z^b=u^b+v^b,\qquad
 T_\alpha^b=\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}D_j^{\alpha_j}.
 \label{eq:veronese-monic}$$ If $A$ is graded and $u,v$ have degree one, these are homogeneous relations when each $T_\alpha$ has degree one; the defining relations <a href="#eq:veronese-exchanges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-exchanges">[eq:veronese-exchanges]</a>–<a href="#eq:veronese-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-linear">[eq:veronese-linear]</a> have degrees two and one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-veronese-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-veronese-extension</summary>

*Proof.* The pair exchanges identify any two products of the same number of symbols whose total exponent vectors agree. To see this directly, regard such a product as an allocation of exponent units among factors, each of total degree $b$. Choose the exponent vector desired in the first factor. Whenever that factor has an excess in one coordinate and a deficit in another, some other factor has a unit in the deficient coordinate. Exchange this unit for a unit in the excess coordinate. This is one relation of the form <a href="#eq:veronese-exchanges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-exchanges">[eq:veronese-exchanges]</a>. Once the first factor has its desired vector, leave it fixed and continue with the remaining factors.

Applying this observation to $T_\alpha^b$ proves the second identity in <a href="#eq:veronese-monic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-monic">[eq:veronese-monic]</a>. In particular, $$v^b=v_T^b=\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}D_j
       =\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}(z-\zeta^j u)=z^b-u^b.$$ Thus $z$ is integral over $A$. Every $D_j$ belongs to $A[z]$, and the second identity in <a href="#eq:veronese-monic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-monic">[eq:veronese-monic]</a> makes every $T_\alpha$ integral over $A[z]$. There are finitely many symbols, so $A'$ is finite over $A$. The grading assertion follows from the displayed defining relations. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-induction-step"></a>
<div id="prop:induction-step" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:new-triangular-row"></a>
**Proposition 29** (The induction step). *Fix $1\le i\le n$. Suppose that a field $k$, a central division algebra $\Delta_0$ over $k$, a formal space $U$, and a low-relation ideal $Q\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_k U$ satisfy the induction conditions of Section <a href="#sec:low-relations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:low-relations">3</a>, with the triangular identities already constructed for the rows $d>i$. Then there are a finitely generated extension $F/k$, a central division algebra $\Delta$ over $F$, and an enlarged formal space and low-relation ideal over $F$ satisfying the same conditions with the rows $d\ge i$ constructed. In particular, the enlarged formal space contains a form $t_i$ for which $$t_i^{a_i}
 =c_i f_i+\sum_{l<i}B_{il}f_l+\sum_{d>i}C_{id}t_d
 \pmod{Q_{\mathrm{new}}},
 \qquad c_i\in F^\times.
 \label{eq:new-triangular-row}$$ All the enlarged formal forms evaluate to mutually commuting linear forms in $\Delta[x]$, and the new relation ideal is generated by linear forms and quadrics.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-induction-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-induction-step</summary>

*Proof.* Put $b=a_i$, let $e_0$ be the degree of $\Delta_0$, and take $\mathfrak j$ and $W$ as in <a href="#eq:stage-ideal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-ideal">[eq:stage-ideal]</a> and <a href="#eq:stage-annihilator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-annihilator">[eq:stage-annihilator]</a>. Write $s=\dim_k W$. Lemma <a href="#lem:stage-basepoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:stage-basepoint">6</a> gives base-point-freeness of $\mathfrak j_b$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:simple-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:simple-factor">7</a> shows that every nonzero geometric member of $W$ has a factor of multiplicity one.

<a id="eq:assembly-h"></a>
<a id="eq:assembly-M"></a>
*Parameters and the invariant sum.* Choose integers in the following order: $$\begin{aligned}
 h&\ge
 \max\left(\{2\}\cup
   \{v_p(e_0)s(p^{v_p(b)}-1):p\mid e_0\}\right),\label{eq:assembly-h}\\
 M&\ge
 \max\left\{2h+4s+10,\ \dim_k\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^b_k U\right\}.
 \label{eq:assembly-M}
\end{aligned}$$ The prime-indexed set in <a href="#eq:assembly-h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-h">[eq:assembly-h]</a> is empty if $e_0=1$. Theorem <a href="#thm:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:parameters">12</a> supplies the geometrically integral projective constraint variety $$X=\mathbb P\left\{(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_M)\in U^{\oplus M}:
           \sum_{m=1}^M w(\ell_m)=0\ \text{for every }w\in W\right\}.$$ Use the curve and its two characters $\chi,\psi$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:binary-curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:binary-curves">13</a>. For $M-1$ copies of the curve, indexed by $m=2,\ldots,M$, let $$H=G^{M-1},\qquad e_1=|H|,$$ and write $\chi_m,\psi_m:H\to\mu_b$ for the corresponding characters. Define characters recursively by <a id="eq:assembly-weights"></a>$$\lambda_M=1,\qquad
 \lambda_{m-1}=\lambda_m\chi_m\quad(M\ge m\ge2),\qquad
 \eta_1=\lambda_1,\quad \eta_m=\lambda_m\psi_m\quad(m\ge2).
 \label{eq:assembly-weights}$$ Let $H$ act on block $m$ of the constraint cone by the point character $\eta_m^{-1}$. This preserves its equations because their degree is $b$, and it gives the natural linearization of $\mathcal O_X(1)$.

<a id="eq:assembly-division"></a>
As in Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a>, put $$B=\prod_{m=2}^M\mathop{\mathrm{Pic}}^0(C),\qquad
 K=k(B\times X\times R),\qquad F=K^H,$$ where $R$ is an affine space with a faithful linear $H$-action. Then $K/F$ is Galois with group $H$. Let $E=\bigotimes_{m=2}^M E_{A_m}$, of dimension $e_1$ over $K$, and let $\Delta_1$ be the descent of $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_K(E)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:division" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:division">18</a>, applied with the block characters just chosen, makes $$\Delta=(\Delta_0\otimes_k F)\otimes_F\Delta_1
 \label{eq:assembly-division}$$ a division algebra. Its scalar extension to $K$ is $\Delta_0\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_K(E)$, and $H$ fixes $\Delta_0$ pointwise. Notice that $e_0,s,b$ were fixed before $h$, and $M$ before $e_1$; there is no restriction involving the new degree $e_1$ in <a href="#eq:assembly-h" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-h">[eq:assembly-h]</a>.

<a id="eq:leaf-weights"></a>
The generic fiber of $\mathcal O_X(1)$, pulled back to $K$, is a one-dimensional semilinear $H$-space. By Galois descent it has a nonzero invariant generator $\tau$. Choose a $k$-basis of $U$. In each cone block, divide its coordinate sections by $\tau$ and use the resulting coefficients to form $\ell_m\in U_K$. Transport of a coordinate section is pullback by $g^{-1}$. The inverse point action $\eta_m^{-1}$ therefore gives $$g\ell_m=\eta_m(g)\ell_m.
 \label{eq:leaf-weights}$$ In particular, dividing by the invariant $\tau$ does not reverse the character.

<a id="eq:invariant-sum"></a>
The constraint equations say that $$S_b:=\sum_{m=1}^M\ell_m^b\in(\mathfrak j_K)_b.$$ Since every $\eta_m^b=1$, the element $S_b$ is invariant and belongs to $(\mathfrak j_F)_b$. We claim that it has a decomposition $$S_b=c_i f_i+\sum_{l<i}B_{il}f_l+\sum_{d>i}C_{id}t_d
          \pmod{Q_F},\qquad c_i\in F^\times.
 \label{eq:invariant-sum}$$ For this, let $$V_b=Q_b+
       \sum_{l<i}f_l\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^{b-a_l}_k U+
       \sum_{d>i}t_d\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^{b-1}_k U.$$ Then $\mathfrak j_b=V_b+kf_i$. If $f_i\in V_b$, any prescribed nonzero coefficient of $f_i$ may be absorbed by changing the term in $V_b$, proving the claim.

Suppose instead that $f_i\notin V_b$. Pure $b$-th powers span $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^b U$ in characteristic zero. Over an algebraic closure, express $f_i$ as a linear combination of at most $\dim\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^b U$ such powers, absorb each scalar into a $b$-th root, and pad with zero forms. The bound <a href="#eq:assembly-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-M">[eq:assembly-M]</a> gives a nonzero cone point with $\sum_m\ell_m^b=f_i$. Thus the homogeneous coefficient of the class of $f_i$ in $\mathfrak j_b/V_b$ is not identically zero on $X$. Geometric integrality makes it nonzero at the generic point. Dividing all the coordinates by $\tau$ only multiplies that coefficient by a nonzero scalar to the $b$-th power. Finally, the spaces $V_b$ and $\mathfrak j_b$ are defined over $k$. Invariant linear algebra over $F$ gives <a href="#eq:invariant-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:invariant-sum">[eq:invariant-sum]</a>, with homogeneous multipliers of the indicated degrees.

<a id="eq:link-substitution"></a>
*The chain of binary additions over $K$.* We next enlarge the formal space over $K$, keeping the old equations. Start with $s'_1=\ell_1$. In link $m$, introduce a fresh symbol $T_{m,\alpha}$ for every degree-$b$ monomial $p^\alpha$ in the curve coordinates. Each symbol has formal degree one. Evaluate it by the multiplication matrix from Proposition <a href="#prop:binary-curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:binary-curves">13</a>, on the $m$-th tensor factor of $E$, with substitution $$U_1=s'_{m-1},\qquad U_2=\ell_m.
 \label{eq:link-substitution}$$ Let $s'_m$ be the formal linear combination of the diagonal symbols corresponding to $z$.

These substitutions preserve commutativity of the entire list. Indeed, the coefficient matrices of a new link commute coefficientwise with $\Delta_0$ and with the earlier tensor factors. The two inputs in <a href="#eq:link-substitution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:link-substitution">[eq:link-substitution]</a> commute with one another and with all earlier forms. The inputs therefore centralize the new coefficient algebra $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_K(E_{A_m})$, embedded in the $m$-th tensor factor. Consequently substitution defines a $K$-algebra homomorphism $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{End}}_K(E_{A_m})[U_1,U_2]
 &\longrightarrow
 \bigl(\Delta_0\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_K(E)\bigr)[x_1,\ldots,x_n],\\
 U_1&\longmapsto s'_{m-1},\qquad U_2\longmapsto\ell_m,
 \end{aligned}$$ where the inputs on the right denote their evaluated forms. It preserves the polynomial commutation identities of the new multiplication matrices, and every new evaluated form commutes with every earlier one. This argument uses no coefficientwise commutativity among the matrices within a single link.

<a id="eq:telescoping-links"></a>
For each link impose the pair-exchange quadrics <a href="#eq:veronese-exchanges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-exchanges">[eq:veronese-exchanges]</a>, the diagonal relations <a href="#eq:veronese-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:veronese-linear">[eq:veronese-linear]</a>, and the identifications $$u_T=s'_{m-1},\qquad v_T=\ell_m.$$ They are identities under evaluation because they are identities of sections on the curve. Lemma <a href="#lem:veronese-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:veronese-extension">28</a> shows that the resulting quotient is finite over the preceding formal relation algebra and gives $$(s'_m)^b=(s'_{m-1})^b+\ell_m^b.$$ After all links, the formal quotient is finite over $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}_K U_K/Q_K$, is defined by the old and new linear and quadratic relations, and satisfies $$(s'_M)^b=\sum_{m=1}^M\ell_m^b=S_b.
 \label{eq:telescoping-links}$$

*Equivariance and descent.* Give the new symbol space in link $m$ the degree-$b$ action on the $p$-monomials from Proposition <a href="#prop:binary-curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:binary-curves">13</a>, multiplied by the character $\lambda_m$, and extend it semilinearly over $K$. Keep $U$ fixed. The pair-exchange relations are stable under the monomial phase and permutation action and under the common twist. The diagonal relations are stable as well. The distinguished symbols $u_T,v_T,z$ have respective weights $$\lambda_m\chi_m=\lambda_{m-1},\qquad
 \lambda_m\psi_m=\eta_m,\qquad
 \lambda_m.$$ These are the weights of their prescribed inputs and output: the input identifications are stable by induction, starting from <a href="#eq:leaf-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leaf-weights">[eq:leaf-weights]</a>. Thus the full ideal of linear and quadratic relations is stable.

Evaluation is equivariant, not merely the relation ideal. For a section $a$ before the twist, write $M_a(U_1,U_2)$ for its linear multiplication matrix. The transport identity <a href="#eq:matrix-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-transport">[eq:matrix-transport]</a> reads $$M_{g_*a}(U_1,U_2)
 =(g_{\mathrm{coeff}}M_a)
      \bigl(\chi_m(g)U_1,\psi_m(g)U_2\bigr),$$ where $g_{\mathrm{coeff}}$ includes the field action and transport on the corresponding endomorphism factor. Scalar choices in transporting the universal line cancel on these endomorphisms. Using the weights of the two inputs and the degree-one homogeneity of the matrix polynomial, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 g\!\left(M_a(s'_{m-1},\ell_m)\right)
 &=(g_{\mathrm{coeff}}M_a)
       \bigl(\lambda_m(g)\chi_m(g)s'_{m-1},
             \lambda_m(g)\psi_m(g)\ell_m\bigr)\\
 &=\lambda_m(g)\,M_{g_*a}(s'_{m-1},\ell_m).
\end{aligned}$$ This is exactly the action assigned to the twisted symbol. It proves equivariance successively through all the links.

Take invariant vector spaces under semilinear Galois descent. The enlarged formal space descends to an $F$-space containing $U_F$, and the full degree-one and degree-two pieces of the stable relation ideal descend to finite-dimensional spaces over $F$. Let $Q_{\mathrm{new}}$ be the ideal generated by these descended spaces. Scalar extension commutes with the multiplication maps generating this ideal. Since the original relation ideal was generated in degrees one and two, extension to $K$ recovers exactly that ideal. Equivariant evaluation takes these invariant forms into $\Delta[x]$, with $\Delta$ as in <a href="#eq:assembly-division" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:assembly-division">[eq:assembly-division]</a>. They are mutually commuting.

The last output $s'_M$ is invariant because $\lambda_M=1$; define $t_i=s'_M$. Combining <a href="#eq:invariant-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:invariant-sum">[eq:invariant-sum]</a> with <a href="#eq:telescoping-links" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:telescoping-links">[eq:telescoping-links]</a> proves <a href="#eq:new-triangular-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:new-triangular-row">[eq:new-triangular-row]</a>. All earlier equations are retained, so every old triangular identity continues to hold modulo $Q_{\mathrm{new}}$.

It remains to check the other induction conditions. Finiteness of the new relation algebra over the old one can be checked after the faithfully flat extension $F\subset K$, where it was proved link by link. In particular it remains finite over $F[x]$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sparsity-preserved" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sparsity-preserved">10</a> now preserves the geometric no-small-curve condition: the linear projection of the new characteristic set is finite and pulls back the old hyperplane bundle. The central variables still evaluate to themselves, and the formal space still contains $U_F$. Finally $K$ is finitely generated over $k$, and so is its finite-group invariant field $F$. Thus the center is still finitely generated over the original $\mathbb C$, and all induction conditions have been verified. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-induction-step-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-induction-step</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:construction">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:initial-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:initial-data">9</a> supplies the initial data with no triangular rows yet constructed. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:induction-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:induction-step">29</a> in the order $i=n,n-1,\ldots,1$. Every step retains all preceding identities as consequences of an ideal generated in degrees at most two. Thus a later, smaller value of $a_i$ does not lose an earlier power identity. After finitely many steps we have the division algebra and commuting linear forms satisfying <a href="#eq:triangular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:triangular">[eq:triangular]</a> for all $i$. Let $A$ be the commutative graded algebra generated by all the evaluated formal forms. The center is finitely generated over $\mathbb C$, all multipliers in the triangular identities belong to $A$ in the stated degrees, and all $c_i$ are nonzero. Lemma <a href="#lem:ordered-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ordered-basis">4</a> gives finiteness over $k[x]$ and the full ordered left-$\Delta$ basis. This proves every assertion of Theorem <a href="#thm:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:construction">1</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-induction-step-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-induction-step</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a>.* Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:construction">1</a> to the regular sequence contained in $I$. Proposition <a href="#prop:monomial-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:monomial-reduction">5</a> gives one monomial ideal $J$ containing all $x_i^{a_i}$ and having the same Hilbert function as $I$ in every degree. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Generality and consequences

The construction and monomial extraction prove Artinian EGH over $\mathbb C$. We first extend that Hilbert-function conclusion to the scope of Corollary <a href="#cor:characteristic-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:characteristic-zero">3</a>, and then derive two further consequences. These arguments use the EGH conclusion rather than any additional division-algebra construction.

<a id="subsec:generality"></a>
#### Regular sequences over characteristic-zero fields

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-induction-step-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-induction-step</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:characteristic-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:characteristic-zero">3</a>.* If $I=S_F$, take $J=S_F$. Otherwise, first work over $\mathbb C$. Choose a linear coordinate change for which $f_1,\ldots,f_c,x_{c+1},\ldots,x_n$ is regular. The images $\overline f_1,\ldots,\overline f_c$ in $\mathbb C[x_1,\ldots,x_c]$ form a full regular sequence. Corollary <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">2</a> applies to these images and, more generally, to every full regular sequence of the prescribed degrees in $c$ variables and every homogeneous ideal containing it. This universal assertion is the premise of the Artinian reduction of Caviglia–Maclagan <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[CM08](#ref-CM08), Proposition 10], which gives the desired Hilbert-function comparison for every ideal containing the original sequence in $n$ variables. The lexicographic embedding modulo $P_F$ gives the stated lex-plus-powers choice; this reduction and its lex formulation are also recalled in <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[CK14](#ref-CK14), Section 4].

For general $F$, choose homogeneous generators of $I$ and let $F_0\subseteq F$ be the subfield generated over $\mathbb Q$ by their coefficients and those of the $f_i$. Let $I_0\subseteq F_0[x]$ be generated by those generators together with the $f_i$. Then $I_0F[x]=I$, and faithful flatness of $F/F_0$ descends regularity of the sequence to $F_0[x]$. Embed the finitely generated field $F_0$ into $\mathbb C$ and apply the complex case after scalar extension. Define a monomial ideal over $F_0$, and then over $F$, using the same monomial exponents as the resulting complex ideal. Dimensions of graded pieces commute with both field extensions from $F_0$, so the Hilbert equalities descend to $F$. The same observation identifies the degreewise lex-plus-powers choice. ◻

</details>

The companion paper proves the stronger graded Betti inequalities in this same characteristic-zero scope <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[BettiCompanion](#ref-BettiCompanion), Corollary 1.2], using the Artinian LPP reduction of Caviglia–Kummini <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[CK14](#ref-CK14), Theorem 4.1]. The construction in Theorem <a href="#thm:construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:construction">1</a> itself remains the stated construction for a full complex regular sequence.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Local cohomology

Caviglia–Sbarra’s conditional theorem <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[CavigliaSbarra13](#ref-CavigliaSbarra13), Theorem 4.4] also gives a local-cohomology comparison for the same lex-plus-powers ideal.

<a id="stmt-cor-local-cohomology"></a>
<div id="cor:local-cohomology" class="corollary">

**Corollary 30** (Lex-plus-powers extremality for local cohomology). *Let $F$ be a field of characteristic zero, let $S_F=F[x_1,\ldots,x_n]$ be standard graded, and put $\mathfrak m=(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$. Let $$1\le c\le n,\qquad 2\le a_1\le\cdots\le a_c,
 \qquad P_F=(x_1^{a_1},\ldots,x_c^{a_c}).$$ Suppose that a homogeneous ideal $I\subseteq S_F$ contains a homogeneous regular sequence $f_1,\ldots,f_c$ with $\deg f_k=a_k$. Let $J$ be the lex-plus-powers ideal prescribed by Corollary <a href="#cor:characteristic-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:characteristic-zero">3</a> for $P_F$, the order $x_1>\cdots>x_n$, and the Hilbert function of $S_F/I$. For this single ideal, $$\dim_F H^i_{\mathfrak m}(S_F/I)_j
 \le
 \dim_F H^i_{\mathfrak m}(S_F/J)_j
 \qquad(i\ge0,\ j\in\mathbb Z).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-local-cohomology"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-local-cohomology</summary>

*Proof.* If $I=S_F$, both sides vanish. Otherwise, since $F$ is infinite, complete $f_1,\ldots,f_c$ to a regular sequence by $n-c$ linear forms. After a linear change of coordinates, the images $\overline f_1,\ldots,\overline f_c$ in $\overline S=F[x_1,\ldots,x_c]$ form a full regular sequence of the same degrees. The list of added linear forms is empty when $c=n$.

Corollary <a href="#cor:characteristic-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:characteristic-zero">3</a>, applied in $\overline S$ to every homogeneous ideal containing this sequence, supplies precisely the Artinian EGH premise of Caviglia–Sbarra <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[CavigliaSbarra13](#ref-CavigliaSbarra13), Theorem 4.4]. Their Clements–Lindström image of $I/(f_1,\ldots,f_c)$ in $S_F/P_F$ has preimage $J$ in $S_F$. The base-independence identification used there gives local cohomology with support in $\mathfrak m$, and their Hilbert-series comparison is coefficientwise in the full internal $\mathbb Z$-grading for every cohomological degree $i\ge0$. This gives the displayed inequalities directly over $F$. ◻

</details>

When $c<n$, this statement includes positive-dimensional quotients. When $c=n$, both quotients are Artinian: their zeroth local cohomology is the quotient itself and their higher local cohomology vanishes, so the comparison reduces to the Hilbert-function equality.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Quadratic Cayley–Bacharach

The quadratic case also gives the following scheme-theoretic Cayley–Bacharach consequence.

<a id="stmt-cor-quadratic-cayley-bacharach"></a>
<div id="cor:quadratic-cayley-bacharach" class="corollary">

**Corollary 31** (Quadratic Cayley–Bacharach). *Let $F$ have characteristic zero, let $r\ge1$, and let $\Omega\subseteq\mathbb P^r_F$ be a zero-dimensional scheme-theoretic complete intersection of $r$ quadrics, of degree $2^r$. For an integer $1\le k\le r$ and a degree-$k$ hypersurface $X\subseteq\mathbb P^r_F$, $$\Omega\not\subseteq X
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \deg(\Omega\cap X)\le 2^r-2^{r-k}.$$ Equivalently, if $X$ contains a closed subscheme of $\Omega$ of degree strictly greater than $2^r-2^{r-k}$, then it contains $\Omega$ scheme-theoretically. Degrees here mean scheme lengths over $F$; no reducedness assumption is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-quadratic-cayley-bacharach"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-quadratic-cayley-bacharach</summary>

*Proof.* First let $m\ge0$ and let $\Gamma\subseteq\Omega$ fail to impose independent conditions on degree-$m$ forms, so that $H_\Gamma(m)<\deg\Gamma$, where $H_\Gamma(d)=\dim_F(S/I_\Gamma)_d$, $S=F[x_0,\ldots,x_r]$, and $I_\Gamma$ is the saturated homogeneous ideal. Choose a linear form $\ell$ nonvanishing on $\Omega$. It is a nonzerodivisor on the homogeneous coordinate rings of both $\Omega$ and $\Gamma$. Thus the images of the defining quadrics form a regular sequence in $S/(\ell)\cong F[x_1,\ldots,x_r]$, and, for $A=S/(I_\Gamma,\ell)$, $$\deg\Gamma=\sum_{j\ge0}\dim_F A_j,
 \qquad H_\Gamma(m)=\sum_{j=0}^m\dim_F A_j.$$ Some $A_e$ is therefore nonzero with $e\ge m+1$. Corollary <a href="#cor:characteristic-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:characteristic-zero">3</a> replaces $A$ by a quotient by a monomial ideal containing every variable square, with the same Hilbert function. A surviving degree-$e$ monomial is squarefree, and all its $2^e$ divisors survive. Consequently $\deg\Gamma\ge2^e\ge2^{m+1}$. This proves, in characteristic zero, the numerical assertion $\mathrm{IV}_m$ of <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[EGH93](#ref-EGH93), Section 3].

Now suppose $X$ does not contain $\Omega$ and $k\le r-1$. Put $Z=\Omega\cap X$, and let $\Gamma$ be residual to $Z$ in $\Omega$. If $I_Z$ and $I_\Omega$ are their saturated homogeneous ideals, the residual scheme is defined by $I_\Gamma=(I_\Omega:I_Z)$. Set $m=r-k-1$. Residual duality for the complete intersection of $r$ quadrics gives $$\deg\Gamma-H_\Gamma(m)
   =\dim_F(I_Z/I_\Omega)_k>0.$$ The equality is the modern Cayley–Bacharach theorem in <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[EGH93](#ref-EGH93), Section 3, p. 195]; its complementary degrees sum to $\sum_{i=1}^r2-r-1=r-1$. The strict inequality holds because the defining equation of $X$ belongs to $I_Z$ but not to $I_\Omega$. This theorem may be applied over an algebraic closure: flat field extension preserves lengths, Hilbert functions, residual schemes, and scheme containment. Thus $\Gamma$ fails to impose independent degree-$m$ conditions. The bound just proved and the residual degree identity give $$\deg(\Omega\cap X)=2^r-\deg\Gamma
 \le 2^r-2^{r-k}.$$ For $k=r$, the same inequality merely says that a proper closed subscheme of $\Omega$ has integer degree at most $2^r-1$. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The induction separates the geometry of the constraints from the binary addition matrices. The return arrow preserves the hypotheses for the next row; monomial extraction is performed only after the construction is complete.",
      "figure_id": "fig:construction",
      "pdf_page": 6,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:construction",
          "exact_quote": "The induction separates the geometry of the constraints from the binary addition matrices. The return arrow preserves the hypotheses for the next row; monomial extraction is performed only after the construction is complete.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n box/.style={draw=linkblue!70,rounded corners=2pt,fill=linkblue!3,align=center,text width=38mm,minimum height=12mm,font=\\small,inner sep=4pt},\n arrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},semithick,draw=linkblue!80},\n every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n\\node[box] (low) at (0,2.1) {Low-degree relations\\\\and no small curves};\n\\node[box] (param) at (5,2.1) {Constraint space $X$\\\\with highly connected\\\\punctured smooth cone};\n\\node[box] (curves) at (0,0) {Binary addition\\\\matrices on curves};\n\\node[box] (division) at (5,0) {Division algebra\\\\extension};\n\\node[box] (row) at (10,0) {New triangular\\\\power identity};\n\\node[box] (ideal) at (10,-2.1) {Ordered basis and\\\\one monomial ideal};\n\\draw[arrow] (low)--(param);\n\\draw[arrow] (param)--(division);\n\\draw[arrow] (curves)--(division);\n\\draw[arrow] (division)--(row);\n\\draw[arrow] (row)-- node[right,align=left]{after all\\\\rows} (ideal);\n\\draw[arrow] (row.north) -- (10,3.2) -- node[above]{finite extension; retain low-degree relations} (0,3.2) -- (low.north);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A flow diagram showing dependencies within an induction step.\nTop row contains two rectangular boxes:\n- Left box: \"Low-degree relations and no small curves\"\n- Right box: \"Constraint space $X$ with highly connected punctured smooth cone\"\nAn arrow points from the left box to the right box.\nBottom row contains three rectangular boxes:\n- Left box: \"Binary addition matrices on curves\"\n- Middle box: \"Division algebra extension\"\n- Right box: \"New triangular power identity\"\nHorizontal arrows point from \"Binary addition matrices on curves\" to \"Division algebra extension\", and from \"Division algebra extension\" to \"New triangular power identity\".\nFrom \"New triangular power identity\", a vertical downward arrow labeled \"after all rows\" points to a bottom box:\n- Bottom box: \"Ordered basis and one monomial ideal\"\nA feedback arrow originates from the right side of \"New triangular power identity\", goes up and loops back across the top labeled \"finite extension; retain low-degree relations\", and points down into the top of \"Low-degree relations and no small curves\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The curve $C$ is the normalization of the fiber product. The lower cover is totally ramified at $0, 1, 2, ∞$; its ramification at $0, 1, ∞$ cancels that of the right-hand cover, making the left-hand cover étale.",
      "figure_id": "fig:curve-covers",
      "pdf_page": 21,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:curve-covers",
          "exact_quote": "The curve $C$ is the normalization of the fiber product.\nThe lower cover is totally ramified at $0,1,2,\\infty$; its ramification\nat $0,1,\\infty$ cancels that of the right-hand cover, making the\nleft-hand cover \\'etale.",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n\\node (C) at (0,2) {$C$};\n\\node (Cp) at (5,2) {$C'$};\n\\node (T) at (0,0) {$T_0$};\n\\node (P) at (5,0) {$\\PP^1_t$};\n\\draw[->] (C)--node[above]{degree $b$}(Cp);\n\\draw[->] (C)--node[left]{\\'etale $G$-cover}(T);\n\\draw[->] (Cp)--node[right]{degree $e=b^b$}(P);\n\\draw[->] (T)--node[below]{degree $b$}(P);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A commutative square diagram with four vertices and four directed arrows:\n- Top-left vertex: $C$\n- Top-right vertex: $C'$\n- Bottom-left vertex: $T_0$\n- Bottom-right vertex: $\\mathbb{P}^1_t$\nArrows and labels:\n- Horizontal arrow from $C$ to $C'$, labeled above with \"degree $b$\"\n- Vertical arrow from $C$ to $T_0$, labeled on the left with \"étale $G$-cover\"\n- Vertical arrow from $C'$ to $\\mathbb{P}^1_t$, labeled on the right with \"degree $e = b^b$\"\n- Horizontal arrow from $T_0$ to $\\mathbb{P}^1_t$, labeled below with \"degree $b$\""
    }
  ],
